Let me analyze the task. This is System F with Nat and primitive recursion, CBV semantics. We need to prove strong normalization.

The key challenge: System F with cbv + Nat + primitive recursion. This is a standard logical relations proof, but the tricky part is the `ST_RecSucc` rule:

```
tm_natrec (tm_succ n) b s --> tm_app (tm_app s n) (tm_natrec n b s)
```

Note: this requires `numeric_value n`, `value b`, `value s`. Interesting: the recursion step applies s to n and to natrec n b s. Since this is System T-style primitive recursion, it's strongly normalizing (Gödel's System T).

However the logical relation needs to handle the recursive case. Standard approach: use a "hereditarily strongly normalizing" interpretation, but for Nat we need something stronger: the term must be SN and all its successors/reducts "compute" appropriately.

Actually the standard approach for System T with primitive recursion at type Nat: interpret Nat as the set of terms t such that t is SN, and every t' reachable (in one step or reducts) that is numeric_value ... hmm, actually for primitive recursion at any type, we need the recursor applied to a numeral to be SN.

Standard technique: define the logical relation as reducibility candidates, and for Nat, require t ∈ Nat iff t is SN and every t' with t -->* t' that is a numeric value... Actually a cleaner way: define Nat as: t is SN, and all t' reachable from t by one step are in Nat, and... Hmm we need: if t ∈ Nat and b ∈ C, s ∈ Nat → C → C, then natrec t b s ∈ C. But t ∈ Nat doesn't say t is a numeral; it could be a stuck term like a free variable. Hmm, but we can prove SN by induction on the SN derivation of t plus term structure? Actually a standard trick:

For the recursion case, define the interpretation of Nat as "strongly normalizing AND (t reduces to a numeral implies ...)". Actually, we want the recursor natrec n b s to be SN when n is SN and b, s are SN (plus s applied appropriately). Consider: the reductions of natrec n b s either reduce n (finitely many, since n SN), or when n is a numeral, reduce b, or reduce s, or take the ST_RecSucc step producing tm_app (tm_app s n') (tm_natrec n' b s), which is a redex structure.

Hmm, the issue: to show natrec n b s is SN when n is a numeral (say numeral k), we can do induction on k, but the resulting term `(s numeral_{k-1}) (natrec numeral_{k-1} b s)` requires knowing s applied to two arguments is SN — this needs s ∈ Nat → (C → C) with the appropriate closure.

Standard "reducibility candidates" approach (Girard's proof for System T): Interpret Nat as SN terms of type Nat, and prove by induction on the SN derivation that all "computable" terms of type Nat satisfy: natrec n b s is computable under the appropriate conditions. There's a classic trick: define a predicate on SN derivations. But simpler: use the "hereditary" definition where Nat is interpreted as terms t such that t is SN and for every reduct... hmm.

Alternative: a well-known clean approach is via a "semantic" formulation where we prove a lemma:

Lemma natrec_strong : forall n b s, strongly_normalizing n -> strongly_normalizing b -> strongly_normalizing s -> (for all numerals? ) ...

Consider the actual operational behavior: CBV. For natrec:
- if n is a numeral (i.e., numeric_value), and b, s values... Actually the rules allow b non-value to reduce (ST_RecBase), s non-value to reduce (ST_RecStep) only after n numeric and b value.
- ST_RecZero requires both b and s values.
- ST_RecSucc requires n numeric, b and s values.

So actually to evaluate natrec n b s: reduce n to a numeral, then reduce b to a value, then reduce s to a value, then if n = zero return b else expand.

Key: the ST_RecSucc step goes to `tm_app (tm_app s n) (tm_natrec n b s)` where n is the predecessor numeral. Now, in the target type C, we have s : Nat → C → C. So `s n : C → C` and then applied to `natrec n b s : C`.

The standard approach: define `C_T` = reducibility candidate for each T. For Nat: the set of SN terms (or the standard candidate). Then prove the fundamental lemma needs: if n ∈ C_Nat, b ∈ C_T, s ∈ C_{Nat→T→T}, then natrec n b s ∈ C_T.

- C_Nat(t) := sn t

Hmm, we need this in general. The natural approach: prove by *well-founded induction on the multiset/lexicographic combination* of (sn n's height, sn s's height?) Hmm.

Alternative Approach B: Use "hereditarily strongly normalizing" where Nat is interpreted as SN terms, and *strengthen the statement* of the fundamental theorem to include closure under recursion. Specifically, define:

`lr : ty -> tm -> Prop` recursively as above with Nat = SN, and prove the fundamental theorem by induction on typing derivation. For T_Rec we need: sn(n) → lr T b → lr (Nat→T→T) s → lr T (natrec n b s). Prove this by ... induction on the derivation of sn n? The definition of sn is an inductive with one constructor: sn t := (∀ u, t --> u → sn u) → sn t. Induction on sn n gives: for all u, n --> u → sn u, and the IH for each u: "for all b s, lr T b → lr (Nat→T→T) s → lr T (natrec u b s)"? But that requires the IH to be generalized over b and s, which we can do by induction on the SN derivation with the statement:

P(n) := ∀ b s, lr T b → lr (Nat→T→T) s → lr T (natrec n b s).

Can we prove ∀ n, sn n → P(n)? We'd do induction on sn n. Given n with all one-step reducts satisfying P, need P(n). We need lr T (natrec n b s) which is: sn(natrec n b s) and closure conditions depending on T.

For SN: we need every one-step reduct of natrec n b s to be SN (equivalently P applied?). The one-step reducts:
- natrec n' b s with n --> n': need SN of that; by IH? IH gives lr T (natrec n' b s) and lr implies SN (if all lr include SN). Good.
- If n is numeric_value: natrec n b' s with b --> b' (given s SN): need lr T (natrec n b' s). But our IH on n's SN doesn't help; b changed. We'd need a different induction (on b's SN or on s's). Since n is numeric_value here, we can't get it from IH on sn n directly... unless we do induction on the SN derivations of b and s simultaneously.
- natrec n b s' where s --> s': similar.
- natrec zero b s --> b: need lr T b, which we have.
- natrec (succ k) b s --> (s k)(natrec k b s), requires numeric_value k, value b, value s. Need lr T of that: need lr of the application. s ∈ lr(Nat → T → T) means SN s and ∀ u, lr Nat u → lr (T→T) (s u). Need lr Nat k (k numeric_value → SN k, so yes if lr Nat = SN... SN k holds). Then lr (T→T) (s k): SN and ∀ w, lr T w → lr T ((s k) w). Now apply with w = natrec k b s. Need lr T (natrec k b s) — IH on sn k! Since k is a subterm... Is k's SN derivable from n = succ k's SN? Hmm, sn (succ k) gives all reducts of succ k are SN, but succ k has no reducts unless k reduces. If k is a numeral, sn k must be established separately. Numeric_value k gives sn k? We'd need a lemma: numeric_value k → sn k, provable by induction: numeral implies SN. Yes.

So the induction on sn n is insufficient for the cases where b or s reduce while n stays. Let's see: for the case natrec n b s with n numeric (so n is a numeral) and b --> b': we need lr T (natrec n b' s). We have lr T b and b --> b'. Since lr T b implies... hmm, lr T b implies SN b and the reduct relation? For candidates, lr T b and b --> b' implies lr T b' (CR2). So lr T b' holds. But we still need to apply the natrec lemma with the new b' — the induction must be on something that decreases. If we do induction on the SN derivation of b, then b --> b' decreases it, but then the natrec expansion case requires going back to n's structure. So we need a *lexicographic* or *simultaneous* induction combining sn n, sn b, sn s.

Standard technique: prove a lemma by induction on the SN derivation of the *term* `natrec n b s` itself? That's circular but maybe we can establish SN of natrec n b s first by a well-founded argument, then get lr?

Hmm, here's a cleaner classic approach: prove SN of natrec via a *direct* well-founded induction using a measure based on the "type level" and the SN heights of n, b, s, where at the ST_RecSucc step the predecessor numeral k has a smaller "numeral size" than n. Let's attempt:

Define the lemma:

Lemma sn_natrec : forall T n b s,
  wf/lc conditions? 
  sn n -> sn b -> sn s -> 
  (forall ... type structure) -> sn (natrec n b s).

Proof by induction on n? n is a term, not a nat. But n's SN derivation: does the ST_RecSucc step produce natrec k b s with k a *subterm-relation*? k is the predecessor of numeral n; n = succ k, so k is a subterm of n. But induction on the SN derivation of n doesn't give k smaller (since succ k is a value with no reductions).

Better: induction on the *numeric_value* structure of n when n is numeric. So we need a case analysis: given sn n, either n is a value that is numeric_value (then n is a numeral by induction), or n is neutral (then all reducts come from n reducing).

Hmm, but terms of type Nat can also be applications that are stuck? In CBV with the given step relation, a term like `tm_app (tm_abs T t) v` always steps. A closed well-typed term of Nat is either a numeral or reducible... but again we're proving SN not progress.

Key idea: do induction on the multiset {sn-height of n, sn-height of b, sn-height of s}? At the ST_RecSucc case we need natrec k b s where k is a subterm of n: if n is numeric_value, then k is structurally smaller than n, and we might have sn k provable. But the "height" of sn k could be larger than sn n? Actually numeral k's SN derivation is linear. Hmm, but syntactically k is smaller than succ k. Structural size decreases! So induction on the structural size of n? But n can reduce (n --> n') without decreasing structural size. Hmm.

Let me think about a well-founded measure that handles both n reducing and the recursion-expansion. This is exactly why the logical relation's Nat interpretation should be "SN + closure properties" so the induction is on the SN derivation, plus the type-indexed closure gives what's needed at the expansion.

Let me revisit. Standard System T normalization proofs in Coq often use the following definition due to Girard:

For each type T, define the set C_T of "computable" terms:
- C_Nat = SN (the set of strongly normalizing terms)
- C_{A→B} = { t | t ∈ SN and ∀u ∈ C_A, t u ∈ C_B }
- C_{∀A} = { t | t ∈ SN and ∀ U lc, t[U] ∈ C_{A[U]} }

And then the key lemma is proved using Girard's "reducibility candidate" machinery: instead of proving natrec ∈ C_T directly by induction, one shows that "computability" is closed under the recursion *rule* by induction on the *type T* plus induction on the SN derivation of n etc.

Actually wait. I recall a neat trick: define the interpretation of Nat as the set of *all* SN terms, and then prove:

Lemma computable_natrec : forall T n b s, 
   lc? -> 
   sn n -> lr T b -> lr (Nat→T→T) s -> lr T (natrec n b s).

Proof by induction on T (the type)! Since T is a type, we can do induction on the structure of T. But Nat case needs SN(natrec n b s) and the arrow case needs the application closure. Hmm, in the arrow case T = A→B, we need lr (A→B) (natrec n b s), which is sn plus ∀u ∈ C_A, lr B (natrec n b s u). To prove lr B (natrec n b s u) we can use the IH for B. But we need SN of natrec n b s (for the first conjunct), which in the arrow case is... a subgoal of sn. Hmm, but sn of natrec n b s doesn't follow from sn of natrec n b s u.

Alternatively, maybe induction on T isn't the way; maybe there's a cleverer trick: prove sn(natrec n b s) using the fact that "natrec n b s" is SN iff all its reducts are SN, and induct on sn n, sn b, sn s.

Let me think carefully about proving sn(natrec n b s) with hypotheses:
- sn n
- lr T b (hence sn b)
- lr (Nat→T→T) s (hence sn s)
- maybe well-typedness? Not needed if the argument is purely combinatorial? Hmm.

The hard case: n numeric_value (thus numeral), b value, s value, n = succ k, step to (s k)(natrec k b s). Need SN of that. We know lr (Nat→T→T) s and lr Nat k (since numeral k is SN) so lr (T→T) (s k). Then we need lr T (natrec k b s) to conclude via lr (T→T) definition... but wait, lr (T→T) (s k) gives ∀ w, lr T w → lr T ((s k) w). So with w = natrec k b s and lr T (natrec k b s), we get lr T ((s k) (natrec k b s)), hence SN. So we need lr T (natrec k b s) where k is the predecessor of numeral n.

So if we can get lr T (natrec k b s) from lr T (natrec (succ k) b s)? That's circular. But note: k is structurally smaller than n, and n is a numeral. So induction on the *external* structure of the numeral works: define a lemma by induction on k:

Lemma natrec_numeral : forall k b s T, lr T b -> lr (Nat→T→T) s -> lr T (natrec (numeral k) b s).

But n is not necessarily the literal term `numeral k`; it's an arbitrary term that happens to be numeric_value. But numeric_value is exactly `numeral k` syntactically! numeric_value tm_zero (numeral 0) and nv_succ: numeric_value n -> numeric_value (tm_succ n). So numeric_value n ↔ exists k, n = numeral k. Yes! numeric_value is exactly the numerals. Great.

So we can do a well-founded induction on... what measure? We need to handle n reducing (sn n), b reducing, s reducing, and numeral structure. I think we can structure the proof as:

Main Lemma L: forall n b s T, sn n -> lr T b -> lr (Nat→T→T) s -> lr T (natrec n b s).

Proof by strong induction on the *triple*? Let's think: define the relation "sn n" and use well-founded induction on the pair (sn-height n + ...)? We don't have heights. We can use `well_founded` induction on the triple lexicographic (n via sn, b via sn, s via sn)? But the case where n is a numeral and we expand to natrec k b s: k is a *subterm* of n, and sn k is derivable but does the induction measure decrease? Let me look at the actual standard proof for STLC/System F normalization (Tait's method). In Tait's method, the interpretation is:
- C_Nat = SN
- C_{A→B} = { t | SN t ∧ ∀ u ∈ C_A, t u ∈ C_B }
And the key lemma is: for a term t of arrow type, if t is SN and all its reducts are in C_{A→B}, then... Hmm, this is the standard "CR" property but usually stated: 

Standard approach (Girard): C is a reducibility candidate: CR1 ⊆ SN, CR2 closed under reduction, CR3 if neutral and all reducts in C then in C. And crucially, **all terms of the form λx.t are in C_{A→B} if their body is** — handled by the beta step analysis. And **applications**: t u ∈ C_B when t ∈ C_{A→B} and u ∈ C_A — by definition. For terms like redexes, they're not neutral, but you don't need CR3 for them because you handle them directly in the fundamental theorem (T_App: t1 t2 ∈ C_B by definition of C_{A→B} applied to t1). The T_Abs case: λx.body ∈ C_{A→B} requires ∀u ∈ C_A, (λx.body) u ∈ C_B. Now (λx.body) u is a redex, and we need to show it's in C_B. Its reduct is body[u/x] ∈ C_B by the IH. So we need: if t --> t' and t' ∈ C_B and t is SN... then t ∈ C_B? This is the "expansion" property. For CRs, the standard lemma is: if t is neutral and all reducts are in C then t ∈ C. For a redex (not neutral), we can use: t = (λx.body) u, and we know more about it: t's reducts are body[u/x] and possibly (λx.body) u' for u --> u'. If all these are in C and t is SN, is t ∈ C? For C = C_B with B arbitrary, this requires the "candidate" property: 

Actually, there's a general lemma in Tait's method: **C is closed under "if t is SN and all one-step reducts are in C, then t ∈ C"** — this holds if C is defined as a "reducibility candidate" satisfying these conditions, but is it true for C_{A→B}? Let's check: t SN, all reducts in C_{A→B}. Need ∀u ∈ C_A, t u ∈ C_B. Hmm, as noted, if t is a value (λx.body), then t u --> body[u/x]; we'd need body[u/x] ∈ C_B and the closure for the other reducts (t u' for u --> u'). So we need "t u ∈ C_B if all its reducts are in C_B and it's SN", which is the same property at type B — so we can prove the property by induction on the type! Let's check the induction:

**Claim P(T):** For all t, if sn t and (∀ t', t --> t' → lr T t') then lr T t.
Proof by induction on T:
- T = Nat: lr Nat t = sn t ✓.
- T = A→B: need sn t ✓; ∀u, lr A u → lr B (t u). Take u ∈ lr A. sn (t u) — need: sn t, sn u → sn (t u). This is a general lemma (SN of application: if t and u are SN then t u is SN; true because reducts of t u are t' u (t SN by IH) or t u' (u SN); this is provable by nested induction on sn t and sn u). Need all reducts of t u to be lr B:
   - t u --> t' u via ST_App1 (needs lc u): t' ∈ lr (A→B) by hypothesis, so t' u ∈ lr B by definition ✓ (regardless of lc).

But is it true in general? The annotations X vs U affect: (1) lc/value checks. If U is lc, then annotations U are lc at the appropriate depths, and X is always lc. Monotonicity in the hole candidate: if C ⊆ C', is lr_C(T) ⊆ lr_{C'}(T)? For positive occurrences (which is all of System F), yes I'd think: the relation at arrow types is monotone in the domain contravariantly? Let's see: lr_C (A→B) t = sn t ∧ ∀u, lr_C A u → lr_C B (t u). If C ⊆ C', then lr_{C'} A ⊆ lr_C A (assuming A's relation is monotone in C positively... domain is contravariant!). Hmm: if C ⊆ C', then lr_C A u is *weaker*? Let's define monotonicity: C ⊆ C' (as predicates) → lr_C T ⊆ lr_{C'} T? For T = Ty_FVar X: lr_C X = C ⊆ C' ✓. For arrow: need lr_C A u → lr_C B (t u) implies lr_{C'} A u → lr_{C'} B (t u) — by IH (monotone at A and B), lr_{C'} A u implies lr_C A u (contravariance!? no): monotonicity at A says lr_C A ⊆ lr_{C'} A, so lr_{C'} A u doesn't give lr_C A u. Hmm, so the arrow case needs *anti-monotonicity* at the domain. Actually, in a logical relation, all type variable occurrences are *positive* in the sense that the relation is monotone... but the function type A→B has A in a negative position syntactically. However, the relation lr_C (A→B) t = ∀u, lr_C A u → lr_C B (t u) is *monotone in C* overall? Let's check: if C ⊆ C', want: (∀u, lr_C A u → lr_C B (t u)) → (∀u, lr_{C'} A u → lr_{C'} B (t u)). Given u with lr_{C'} A u, we need lr_C A u to apply the hypothesis — but that's anti-monotone at A. So the arrow case requires anti-monotonicity at A, which by induction would require... For a type variable X, lr_C X = C: anti-monotonicity at X would mean C' ⊆ C. Hmm, so the relation is NOT monotone in general? Let's re-derive: logical relations are monotone with respect to the interpretation of type variables because all occurrences are "positive" in the semantic sense: the semantic interpretation of A→B is [A] → [B] where the function space is contravariant in [A]. So if [A]_C ⊆ [A]_C', then the function space [A]→[B] is *anti-monotone* in [A]. But [A]_C ⊆ [A]_C' for C ⊆ C' by induction (for all A). Contravariance then gives: [A→B]_C' ⊆ [A→B]_C? Hmm, careful: [A→B]_C = {t | ∀u ∈ [A]_C, t u ∈ [B]_C}. If [A]_C ⊆ [A]_C' and [B]_C ⊆ [B]_C', take t ∈ [A→B]_C': ∀u ∈ [A]_C', t u ∈ [B]_C'. For u ∈ [A]_C ⊆ [A]_C', t u ∈ [B]_C' — but we need t u ∈ [B]_C, which doesn't follow! So indeed the relation is *anti-monotone* at arrow types? Let's check with a concrete example: C ⊆ C' (predicates on terms). T = X→Nat. [X→Nat]_C t = sn t ∧ ∀u, C u → sn (t u); [X→Nat]_{C'} t = sn t ∧ ∀u, C' u → sn (t u). Since C ⊆ C', the condition with C' is stronger, so [X→Nat]_{C'} ⊆ [X→Nat]_C. So the relation is anti-monotone at X→Nat. And at Nat→X: [Nat→X]_C t = sn t ∧ ∀u, sn u → C (t u) — monotone in C. So the variance flips. So in general, the relation is neither monotone nor anti-monotone; it's "parametric" in C.

Hmm OK. So the monotonicity trick won't work directly.

Let me step back and consider: maybe use the valuation approach but keep it as simple as possible. Actually, here's a thought: maybe we can avoid explicit valuations by **generalizing the type substitution lemma to all types** in the following form:

**Lemma (key)**: For all types T and U, locally closed U, and terms t, and any fresh type variable X (not free in T?):
   lr (open_ty T U) (open_tm_ty t U) ↔ lr (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) (open_tm_ty t X) — is this true with the convention that free type variables are interpreted as SN? We showed a counterexample? Let me construct one: T = Ty_BVar 0, t = tm_abs (Ty_BVar 0) (tm_app (tm_bvar 0) (tm_bvar 0))? Then open_ty T U = U, open_tm_ty t U = abs U (bvar 0 bvar 0). LHS = lr U (abs U (bvar 0 bvar 0)), RHS = lr (Ty_FVar X) (abs X (bvar 0 bvar 0)) = sn (a value) ✓. So LHS could be false (U = Nat→Nat, as computed: ∀u ∈ SN, sn (u u) fails) while RHS true. So the lemma is false with X↦SN. Hence valuation needed.

OK so let's commit to the valuation-based approach. Now, how to structure the development to minimize pain?

Let me define:

```
Definition cand (P : tm -> Prop) : Prop :=
  (forall t, P t -> strongly_normalizing t) /\
  (forall t t', P t -> step t t' -> P t') /\
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> ... )  (* maybe not needed *)
```

Hmm, do we need ρ X to be a candidate? In the relation definition, `lr_ρ (Ty_FVar X) t := ρ X t`. The properties we need of lr_ρ T:
1. sn: lr_ρ T t → sn t. For Ty_FVar X, this requires ρ X ⊆ SN — so yes, candidates.
2. Closure under step: lr_ρ T t → step t t' → lr_ρ T t'. For Ty_FVar X: need ρ X closed under step (CR2).
3. Arrow/All closure.
4. "Head expansion" or neutral closure.

I think the valuation approach is the cleanest. Let me now think carefully about the *exact* formulation that makes the T_TAbs proof go through, and how much work the semantic substitution lemma is.

Standard formulation (from Girard's proof adapted to locally nameless with atoms):

- Let `tyenv := atom -> tm -> Prop` (valuation of type variables as candidates).
- Define `lr (ρ : tyenv) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop` by recursion on T:
  - Ty_BVar i => sn t (or maybe False? shouldn't occur at top level; but occurs under Ty_All which is handled by open_ty, so after opening, no Ty_BVar 0... but arbitrary Ty_BVar i > 0 can occur if the type isn't closed. In well-formed types (wf_ty with context Δ), the bound vars are properly scoped, so `open_ty A U` for wf types has no dangling bvars at top level. But our lr must handle them anyway for Coq's sake. Use sn t.)
  - Ty_FVar X => ρ X t
  - Ty_Arrow A B => sn t ∧ ∀ u, lr ρ A u → lr ρ B (app t u)
  - Ty_All A => sn t ∧ ∀ U, lc_ty U → lr ρ (open_ty A U) (tapp t U)
  - Ty_Nat => sn t.

Wait, in the All case, the opened type `open_ty A U`: its free type variables include those of A (which are interpreted by ρ), and those of U. But U's free type variables should also be interpreted by ρ — that's fine, since ρ is a total function on atoms.

Now, the fundamental theorem: has_type Δ Γ t T → for all ρ, env_rel Γ σ → lr ρ T (σ t).

Hmm, wait: does the statement need Δ at all? The typing relation's Δ is used for wf_ty checks. The logical relation doesn't care about Δ except through lc-ness. But the T_TAbs rule extends Δ with X. The fundamental theorem quantified over all ρ handles it: the premise has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t X) (open_ty T X) holds for fresh X, and the IH gives lr ρ (open_ty T X) (open_tm_ty t X) for all ρ. Choose ρ' = ρ[X ↦ candidate(lr ρ U)]? Hmm, but the relation at `open_ty T X` under ρ' needs to be related to the relation at `open_ty T U` under ρ via the semantic substitution lemma, with the term being `open_tm_ty t X` vs `open_tm_ty t U`.

So the key lemma (semantic type substitution):

**Lemma lr_ty_subst**: For all ρ, X, U (lc), T, t (lc?):
   lr ρ[X ↦ C] T t ↔ lr ρ (ty_subst X U T) (tm_ty_subst X U t),  where C = lr ρ U.

Hmm, in the application, T = open_ty T0 X and t = open_tm_ty t0 X. Then ty_subst X U (open_ty T0 X) = open_ty T0 U? Let's verify the algebra: ty_subst X U (open_ty_rec 0 (Ty_FVar X) T0). Since open_ty_rec substitutes Ty_FVar X for Ty_BVar 0 at depth 0, and ty_subst X U replaces free X by U. They commute: ty_subst X U (open_ty T0 X) = open_ty T0 U (assuming X not free in T0 — which we can ensure by choosing X fresh w.r.t. T0? In the T_TAbs case, X is fresh w.r.t. L (a finite set), and we can choose X fresh w.r.t. T0 as well by enlarging L? But L is given by the typing rule; the premise holds for all X ∉ L. We're free to choose X. We can pick X fresh w.r.t. T0, U, ρ, and L. But wait: the conclusion's relation involves T0's free type variables interpreted by ρ; choosing X fresh w.r.t. T0 is doable since L can be extended? The typing rule T_TAbs gives us: for all X ∉ L, the premise. We need X also not free in T0 and U. Since the premise holds for all X ∉ L, we can pick X ∉ L ∪ FV(T0) ∪ FV(U) ∪ ... — as long as such X exists (infinite atoms) ✓. But actually, the fundamental theorem will be applied to the premise for a chosen X; the premise is universally quantified over X ∉ L so we can instantiate at our chosen X ✓.

Similarly, tm_ty_subst X U (open_tm_ty t0 X) = open_tm_ty t0 U assuming X fresh w.r.t. t0? Hmm, open_tm_ty t0 X substitutes X for bound type var 0 in t0; then substituting X ↦ U gives open_tm_ty t0 U. This holds if X doesn't occur free in t0 or U (avoid capture). We can pick X fresh w.r.t. t0 and U. But wait, the term t0 could contain free type variables; if X is fresh w.r.t. those, fine.

So: choose X fresh w.r.t. L ∪ {free type vars of T0, t0, U, ρ}? We need X fresh so that the algebra works. Since X is a *type variable* (atom), and atoms are nats, we can always pick a fresh one — but "fresh w.r.t. ρ" is weird since ρ is a total function atom → predicate; freshness only matters for the finitely many atoms where ρ matters, i.e., FV(T0) ∪ FV(U). We can pick X not in FV(T0) ∪ FV(U) ∪ L. Then define ρ' := ρ[X ↦ lr ρ U]. Since X is not in FV(T0), and... hmm, we need:
   lr ρ' (open_ty T0 X) (open_tm_ty t0 X)  [from IH with ρ']

For the lemma we need ty_subst X U (open_ty T0 X) = open_ty T0 U and tm_ty_subst X U (open_tm_ty t0 X) = open_tm_ty t0 U, which hold if X ∉ FV(T0) ∪ FV(t0) ∪ FV(U) (need X not free in U to avoid substituting inside U? Actually ty_subst X U substitutes free occurrences of X; if X is free in U, then substituting into U again would be wrong: ty_subst X U (Ty_FVar X) = U requires no further substitution inside U — ty_subst X U U would change U's free X's. The lemma ty_subst X U (open_ty T0 X) = open_ty T0 U needs: open_ty T0 U substitutes U for bound vars; ty_subst X U on open_ty T0 X replaces X's. If X is free in U, the result differs. So require X ∉ FV(U) ✓ pick fresh.)

So the semantic substitution lemma is applied with T containing X free. Hmm, but the lemma as stated has T arbitrary; for T = open_ty T0 X, T contains X possibly (it does, at the hole positions). Then ty_subst X U T = open_ty T0 U ✓.

So the semantic substitution lemma must handle T with X free ✓ (that's the point).

Now let's prove the semantic substitution lemma. Statement:

**Lemma lr_subst_ty**: for all ρ X U (lc U) T t,
   lr (ρ[X ↦ lr ρ U]) T t ↔ lr ρ (ty_subst X U T) (tm_ty_subst X U t).

Wait, there's an issue: `lr ρ U` — for this to be a candidate, U must be locally closed and its free type variables interpreted by ρ. OK.

Proof attempt by induction on T:
- Ty_Nat: both sides sn t vs sn (tm_ty_subst X U t). So we need: sn t ↔ sn (tm_ty_subst X U t) for lc U. Is this true without lc-ness of t?! Hmm. If t is not lc, the annotation substitution could change SN status: e.g., t = abs (Ty_FVar X) (app (bvar 0) (bvar 0)): if X is lc annotation then the abs is a value (if body lc) — body `app (bvar 0) (bvar 0)` is lc at depth k=1 ✓ — so t is a value hence SN. tm_ty_subst X U t = abs U (app (bvar 0) (bvar 0)): also a value (U lc ✓), SN ✓. Same. But consider annotation that is NOT lc: t = abs (Ty_FVar X) (app (bvar 0) (bvar 0)) — lc. Hmm. For sn t ↔ sn (subst t), the concern is when the annotation affects value-ness differently. If U is lc and X is a free type var (lc anywhere), then substituting preserves lc of the annotation, so value-ness of abs/tabs terms is preserved... but only if the *body* is lc, which is unaffected by type substitution? tm_ty_subst changes annotations in the body too, and lc of the body depends on annotations... but each annotation X ↔ U preserves lc. If t is non-lc, then both t and t' are non-lc (by the correspondence), so neither is a value. If t is lc, both are values. So value-ness matches.
  Steps also correspond: t --> t' iff tm_ty_subst X U t --> tm_ty_subst X U t' with the same rule structure, since lc/value checks correspond and open/subst commute. Then SN t ↔ SN t' follows by a bisimulation argument (any infinite reduction on one side maps to the other). This is a standard "SN is preserved by a simulation" lemma. We can prove it with an induction: sn t → sn (tm_ty_subst X U t) plus the reverse direction; each needs the step-correspondence lemma. I think it's provable.

- Ty_FVar Y: LHS = ρ'(Y)(t); RHS = ρ(Y)(tm_ty_subst X U t). We should include that in the definition of candidate: candidates are not just any predicates; they should be "annotation-substitution invariant"?? That's awkward. Hmm. Alternatively, note RHS: ty_subst X U (Ty_FVar Y) = Ty_FVar Y (if Y ≠ X), so RHS = ρ Y (tm_ty_subst X U t) vs LHS = ρ Y t. So we need every candidate ρ Y to satisfy P(t) ↔ P(tm_ty_subst X U t). 

Ugh. That means the class of candidates must be restricted to predicates invariant under type-annotation substitution. That's a strange condition but maybe manageable? Alternative: define lr as an **inductive family** (not a Fixpoint) with the type-level rules, and use the inductive's own induction principle. Let's think:

Inductive lr (ρ : atom -> ty) : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| lr_nat : ...
| lr_var : forall X t, wf? -> lr ρ (ρ X) t -> lr ρ (Ty_FVar X) t
| lr_arrow : ...
...

The `lr_var` constructor refers to `lr ρ (ρ X)` — if this were a *constructor* of an inductive, it's a positive occurrence? `lr ρ (ρ X) t` in a premise — the inductive is indexed by types, and the premise uses an index expression involving ρ X. This is allowed in Coq (nested recursion? no, it's the same inductive at a different index — strictly positive, allowed). So we can define an inductive family:

Inductive lr (ρ : atom -> ty) : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
  | LR_Nat : forall t, sn t -> lr ρ Ty_Nat t
  | LR_Var : forall X t, lr ρ (ρ X) t -> lr ρ (Ty_FVar X) t
  | LR_Arrow : forall A B t, sn t -> (forall u, lr ρ A u -> lr ρ B (tm_app t u)) -> lr ρ (Ty_Arrow A B) t
  | LR_All : forall A t, sn t -> (forall U, lc_ty U -> lr ρ (open_ty A U) (tm_tapp t U)) -> lr ρ (Ty_All A) t
  | LR_BVar : forall i t, sn t -> lr ρ (Ty_BVar i) t.

But this inductive is problematic: LR_Var makes the relation at Ty_FVar X equal to the relation at ρ X — and if ρ is arbitrary, this could loop (ρ X = Ty_FVar X). But we don't need it to be well-founded for the definition (it's an inductive, so it's the least fixed point). However, the induction principle over lr would be weak/not give the closure properties. Hmm, and the fundamental theorem needs to establish lr from typing; with LR_Var we'd need lr ρ (ρ X) t for free type vars, which is not available.

Alternatively, define ρ : atom -> (tm -> Prop) and add the invariance property of candidates. What if we define the class of candidates as *those predicates that are logical relations at some type*? I.e., ρ X := lr ρ0 (some type V_X). Then the invariance lemma at Ty_FVar X reduces to the lemma at type V_X — and V_X is a fixed type, but the lemma is being proven by induction on T, and V_X is unrelated to T. Circular.

Hmm, how do standard proofs handle this? In Girard's proof, the interpretation of a type variable is an arbitrary reducibility candidate, and the key lemma is that the interpretation of any type is a candidate and that "candidate-ness" is preserved under type substitution. The lemma "C_{A[X:=U]}[X:=C_U] = C_A[X:=C_U]" is proven by induction on A, where the Ty_FVar case is: if the variable is X, then C_{X}[X:=C_U] = C_U, and C_{U}[X:=C_U] should equal C_U — is that true in their setup? They interpret type variables by candidates and use the *same* valuation, and when defining C_{∀X.A} they quantify over all candidates C: C_{∀X.A}[ρ] = ∩_{C candidate} C_A[ρ, X↦C]. Then the substitution lemma: C_{A[X:=U]}[ρ] = C_A[ρ, X↦C_U[ρ]] where C_U[ρ] is the candidate of U. This is proven by induction on A, and the variable case: if the variable is X, LHS = C_{U}[ρ], RHS = C_{X}[ρ, X↦C_U[ρ]] = C_U[ρ] ✓ — trivially, because the valuation is *semantic* (assigns candidates) and the substitution is at the level of the *semantics*: in their setup, type expressions are interpreted compositionally, so A[X:=U] with U a type expression is interpreted by evaluating U's semantics. The key difference from our setup: our types are *syntactic* and our relation is defined by recursion on the syntactic type. The substitution lemma in our setting at Ty_FVar X would be: lr ρ' (Ty_FVar X) t ↔ lr ρ (ty_subst X U (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_ty_subst X U t), i.e., C(t) ↔ lr ρ U (tm_ty_subst X U t), where C = lr ρ U. So it reduces to "annotation substitution invariance at type U". In Girard's semantic setup, the terms don't have type annotations, so there's no issue. In our syntactic setting, we need annotation invariance.

So: we need "for all types U (lc), lr ρ U t ↔ lr ρ U (tm_ty_subst X U t)" — annotation substitution invariance at type U. This is provable by induction on U? Let's check: 
- U = Ty_Nat: sn invariance ✓ (needs the SN lemma).
- U = Ty_FVar Y: ρ Y t ↔ ρ Y (tm_ty_subst X U t). Here ρ Y is arbitrary! Circular again — unless ρ's range is restricted.

Hmm. But wait: maybe we don't need full generality. In the application (T_TAbs), the valuation ρ' = ρ[X ↦ lr ρ U] is used only at... hmm, the semantic substitution lemma with T = open_ty T0 X: as we recurse into T0, we may hit free type variables Y of T0, whose ρ Y are arbitrary. So the lemma must hold for arbitrary ρ. So the Ty_FVar Y (Y≠X) case requires candidates to be annotation-invariant.

Options:
(a) Restrict the valuation to annotation-invariant candidates.
(b) Define the valuation as mapping to types: ρ : atom -> ty, with lr (Ty_FVar X) t := lr(ρ X) t defined via... circular unless we index by a well-founded rank.
(c) Prove annotation invariance for *all* lr ρ T predicates as a separate lemma, by induction on T, where the Ty_FVar case uses the hypothesis that ρ Y is annotation-invariant — i.e., include it as a property of candidates and ensure the initial valuation satisfies it and the update ρ[X ↦ lr ρ U] preserves it (which follows from the lemma itself at U).

I think (a)/(c) is workable: define

Definition cand (P : tm -> Prop) : Prop :=
  (forall t, P t -> sn t) ∧
  (forall t t', P t -> step t t' -> P t') ∧
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> ...?) ∧
  (forall X U t, lc_ty U -> P t -> P (tm_ty_subst X U t))? 

Hmm, but then proving that lr ρ T is a candidate requires proving annotation invariance for lr ρ T, which by induction on T hits Ty_FVar Y needing cand(ρ Y) — fine if we assume all ρ Y are candidates. And the update ρ[X ↦ lr ρ U] must be a candidate: need cand (lr ρ U) ✓ from the lemma. But there's a chicken-and-egg for the All case in proving "lr ρ T is a candidate": annotation invariance at Ty_All A requires invariance for arbitrary U (the ⊤ case), which by induction needs invariance at A[U] — not a subterm! Ugh, the All case again.

This is the recurring problem: the All case quantifies over arbitrary U, so structural induction on types fails when we need properties of open_ty A U.

Standard solution in locally nameless: prove the property for *all* types by induction on the type with the All case using... the fact that `open_ty A U` with U fresh variable? Hmm.

Hmm, let me think about how SN proofs for System F in Coq are usually done. Maybe they avoid locally nameless type substitution by using **de Bruijn indices and a "type environment" for the All case**: interpret `Ty_All A` using a *semantic* operation that doesn't involve syntactic substitution, e.g., the All case is defined by quantification over a *type variable valuation* in a *Kripke* style:

lr (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ (ρ' : atom -> ...), ∀ X, ... lr (ρ'[X↦...]) A (tapp t X)?

The term `tapp t U` requires a syntactic type U for the step relation. In locally nameless, `tm_tapp t T` carries the type T. What if the All interpretation quantifies over *α-equivalent* representations: for all types U, all terms t', if tapp t U -->* t' then ... Hmm.

Alternative: maybe we can define the All case *without opening*, by quantifying over a valuation of the bound variable and using a *type-level environment* in the relation:

Define lr : (atom -> tm -> Prop) -> (nat -> tm -> Prop)?? Hmm: interpret de Bruijn type variables via a list/function of candidates too! Then `lr ρ σ T t` where σ : nat → tm → Prop interprets bound variables. The All case: lr ρ σ (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ U lc, ∀ C = (lr ρ σ' U), lr ρ (0 ↦ C; σ↑) A (tapp t U). But the term `tapp t U` carries U, and the relation at A (with bound var 0 ↦ candidate of U) should correspond to the relation at `open_ty A U` (with U's free vars in ρ). Since A is interpreted with bound var 0 as the candidate of U, and open_ty A U is interpreted with U's free vars under ρ — these should agree by a substitution lemma... which is now between the de Bruijn interpretation and the named interpretation. Hmm, still a substitution lemma but maybe simpler? In the de Bruijn setting, `open_ty A U` replaces all Ty_BVar 0 occurrences (at the right depth) with U (with de Bruijn indices shifted? note open_ty_rec doesn't shift U's bound vars — it's a naive substitution that assumes U is closed at the relevant level... U is locally closed, so fine).

Hmm, honestly, we're going to need a type substitution lemma no matter what. Let's try to formulate the development so that the substitution lemma is provable by induction on the type with the All case handled by *the definition itself*:

Define lr by recursion on the type, with the All case:
   lr ρ (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ U lc, lr ρ (open_ty A U) (tapp t U).
The problematic inductive cases are those where we need a property of `lr ρ (open_ty A U)` and can only get it from a property of A. Let's list the properties we need and see if each can be proven by a different route:

Property 1 (SN): lr ρ T t → sn t. Proof by induction on T: All case needs sn (tapp t U) from lr ρ (open_ty A U) (tapp t U) → SN by the "IH" at type open_ty A U — not a subterm. Hmm! But wait: we have `sn t` as a conjunct for All, and sn (tapp t U) follows since t is SN and... if t is a value (tabs), then tapp t U may step to open_tm_ty body U; if t is SN but a tabs, is tapp t U SN? It depends on open_tm_ty body U being SN, which is exactly what lr at A[U] gives via its SN property. So we need the SN property at open_ty A U. Circular-ish but maybe we can do induction on the *type structure* with the All case requiring it for arbitrary U — no.

Alternative: Prove the SN property *simultaneously* with the relation definition in a "stratified" way? Hmm, what if we define lr such that SN is structurally obvious: include sn as a conjunct and prove SN of applications/instantiations using a *separate* induction on SN of the term itself rather than on the type. E.g., to show sn (tapp t U) from lr (All A) t: t is SN. Prove by induction on sn t: if t is not a value, its reducts are t' with lr (All A) t' (closure under step — need property 2), so tapp t U's reducts are tapp t' U, and by IH (sn t') each is SN... but IH at type?? The induction is on sn t with the statement "for all types T, lr T t → ...", so it's fine: we prove a big lemma by induction on sn t. Hmm, but the closure property (lr T t → step t t' → lr T t') itself needs proving.

This suggests a different proof organization: prove all the "candidate" properties by **simultaneous induction on the SN derivation of the term**, with the type being an arbitrary parameter, and use well-founded induction on types only where needed.

Let's think about the classic "Tait's method" proof structure that avoids induction on types:

1. Define lr (semantically) by recursion on type.
2. Prove: for all T, lr T t → sn t. **By induction on the SN derivation of t?** No, the type T is the recursion. Hmm.

Actually, standard proofs do prove "CR1: every computable term is SN" by induction on the *type* (since the definition of computability at arrow types refers to computability at smaller types). The All case is the problem: computability at ∀A refers to A[U] for arbitrary U. In Girard's Tait-style proof, the All case is handled by defining the interpretation with type variables ranging over arbitrary candidates: C_{∀X.A} = ∩_{C ∈ Cand} C_{A}[X:=C]. Then CR1 for C_{∀X.A} requires CR1 for C_A under all valuations; by induction on the type A, CR1 holds for A under any valuation (the valuation affects only type variables; for a type variable X, C_X = the candidate C which is assumed to be a CR). So the induction on A works because the *valuation* handles bound variables — no openness/substitution at the term level!

The catch in our setting: the term operation `tapp t U` requires a syntactic type U, and the candidate for U is `lr ρ U`. So the All case would be: lr ρ (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ U lc, lr (ρ[X↦lr ρ U]) A (tapp t U)?? But A here is a type with a *bound* variable (Ty_BVar 0), not a free variable! In locally nameless, the body A uses de Bruijn indices. So the valuation would need to interpret *de Bruijn* variables: ρ : nat → (tm → Prop). Then:

lr_ρ (Ty_BVar i) t := ρ i t
lr_ρ (Ty_FVar X) t := ρ (some encoding of X)? Hmm, mixing named free vars and de Bruijn bound vars. We could interpret both: a valuation for de Bruijn levels plus free atoms.

For the All case: lr_ρ (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ U lc, lr_{ρ'[0 ↦ lr_ρ U]} A (tapp t U) — where ρ' shifts the de Bruijn valuation under the binder (or we use a list). Hmm, but now the term `tapp t U` is related to type A (with bound variable 0) under the valuation 0 ↦ candidate of U. And the *typing rule* gives us relations at type `open_ty T X` for fresh type variable X — i.e., a type with X free in place of bound var 0. To connect, we need a lemma: lr_{ρ[0↦C]}(A) t ↔ lr_{ρ[X↦C]}(open_ty A X) t for a fresh X... but the terms! The terms in the typing premise have annotations with X (from open_tm_ty t X), whereas the All interpretation needs the term `tapp t U` and its reducts — annotations with U vs X. Ugh, the same annotation issue.

OK, at this point I think the annotation-insensitivity is unavoidable in any approach due to the locally nameless syntax with type annotations in terms. So let's plan to prove it. And the All-case problem for the induction on types: the properties needed for `open_ty A U` can be obtained if we prove the properties for *all* types by... hmm.

Let me look at this from the perspective of the actual literature on formalizing System F SN in Coq. I recall the "Coq: Strong normalization of System F" developments; e.g., in the "Logical Relations" chapter of "Program Logics" or the "TAPL in Coq" work. Also, the "Software Foundations" "Norm" chapter proves normalization for STLC via a logical relation with a Fixpoint on types. For System F, one needs impredicativity; typically the proof uses the *parametric* interpretation of type variables as candidates, and the All case quantifies over all candidates with a valuation. The type substitution lemma is then proven as a separate induction.

- lr (Ty_FVar X) t := ρ_atom X t
- lr (Ty_Arrow A B) t := sn t ∧ ∀u, lr A u → lr B (t u)
- lr (Ty_All A) t := sn t ∧ ∀ U, lc_ty U → lr (ρ_bvar := 0 ↦ lr ρ U; shifted) A (tapp t U).

Hmm, wait: the bound variable in the type A is de Bruijn index 0 (bound by Ty_All). Under the binder, the de Bruijn indices shift. If we use de Bruijn *levels* or *indices*, the valuation manipulation differs. With indices: inside A, the variable bound by Ty_All is index 0, and outer binders are indices ≥1. So when interpreting A under the binder, the valuation should map 0 ↦ candidate of U and (i+1) ↦ ρ_bvar i. ✓ That's the "cons" operation.

Now, the crucial advantage: the All case refers to `lr (cons (lr ρ U) ρ) A (tapp t U)` where A is a *subterm* of Ty_All A ✓. So structural induction on types works!! No type substitution needed for the relation's properties. 

But we still need to connect this with the *typing judgment*, whose T_TAbs rule uses atoms and open_ty. The typing premise gives (for fresh X): has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t X) (open_ty T X). The fundamental theorem would give lr for the type `open_ty T X` under some valuation of atoms/bvars. We need lr for the type `Ty_All T` (the conclusion type) — which under the definition requires ∀U lc, lr ρ[0↦lr U] T (tapp (tabs t) U). The reduct is open_tm_ty t U.

So we need a lemma connecting `lr ρ' T (open_tm_ty t U)` (with de Bruijn valuation ρ' = 0 ↦ lr ρ U) to `lr ρ'' (open_ty T X) (open_tm_ty t U)` (with atom valuation ρ'' = X ↦ lr ρ U). This is the "de Bruijn vs named" correspondence. Both types are "semantically the same" (T with the bound var interpreted as U's candidate). The terms are the same: open_tm_ty t U.

Hmm, but the typing hypothesis gives `open_tm_ty t X`, not `open_tm_ty t U`. So we still need the annotation substitution X→U on the term. Ugh!

Unless... hmm. Is there a way to make the term annotation match? The All interpretation uses `tapp t U`, and the reduct is `open_tm_ty t U` where t is the body *with bound variables*, and open_tm_ty substitutes U for bound type var 0 in the *annotations*. The typing premise's term is `open_tm_ty t X`. So we need to compare `open_tm_ty t X` with `open_tm_ty t U`. Unavoidable. So annotation-insensitivity is needed regardless.

Given that, maybe we can simplify by *defining the relation to be annotation-insensitive by construction*: define the relation on a "type-annotation erasure" of terms! I.e., define `lr T t` in terms of the erased term `erase t` (which drops type annotations), so that annotations are literally ignored. But then the semantics (step) must also be projected to erased terms. The step relation on erased terms: does it correspond? As discussed, for locally closed terms, steps project; but lr quantifies over arbitrary terms (not necessarily lc) in the arrow case. Hmm, but if lr is defined on erased terms with an erased step relation, then we'd have a well-defined relation independent of annotations. Then the fundamental theorem would need: well-typed t → lr T (erase t) → and finally SN of the annotated t. But lr T (erase t) would be about the erased term's reduction, not the annotated term's. The annotated term's reduction might be *different* (if lc checks fail) — but well-typed terms are lc, so the annotated reduction projects to the erased reduction, and SN of the erased term implies SN of the annotated term? If the annotated term's steps project to erased steps, then an infinite annotated reduction gives an infinite erased reduction (possibly with stuttering? projections are steps, so fine). So we'd need: well-typed → lc; and lr T (erase t) → SN_erased (erase t) → SN (t) for lc t.

Hmm! This might actually simplify things: define the relation on erased terms with an erased step relation, and handle everything there, then transfer back. But the erased language still has type abstractions/applications (with no type arguments) and type-erased annotations. The candidate machinery would be over erased terms; the type index still has structure; the All case would quantify over... the erased `tapp` has no type argument, so the instantiation step is just `tapp (tabs b) --> b`, no U needed! And the definition of lr (Ty_All A) t (on erased terms) would need to reflect the body's relation at A with the bound variable... which requires a valuation of the bound type variable by *something*. Hmm, but with erasure, the instantiation doesn't depend on U, so we could define lr (Ty_All A) u := sn u ∧ ∀ (candidate C), lr_{A[C]} ... — the erased term doesn't need U, so we can quantify over candidates directly! That's the Girard-style impredicative interpretation:

lr (Ty_All A) u := sn u ∧ ∀ C (candidate), lr_{ρ[0↦C]} A (tapp u).

This is beautiful: with erased terms, the All case quantifies over arbitrary candidates C, and the relation at A is well-defined by recursion on the type with a valuation of de Bruijn vars by candidates. No type substitution, no annotation issues!

But then the fundamental theorem: well-typed annotated t ↦ erase t. The typing rules use type substitution/opening, but those affect only annotations, which are erased! So has_type Δ Γ t T → lr T (erase t)? Hmm, the typing rules for T_TAbs: conclusion has_type Δ Γ (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T) → need lr (Ty_All T) (erase (tm_tabs t)) = lr (Ty_All T) (etabs (erase t)). Definition: ∀ C candidate, lr_{0↦C} T (etapp (etabs (erase t))) — the erased term `etapp (etabs b)` steps to b (erased beta for type application) ✓. So we'd need lr_{0↦C} T (erase t) for arbitrary C. The typing premise gives (for fresh X) has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t X) (open_ty T X), and by IH, lr (open_ty T X) (erase (open_tm_ty t X)) = lr (open_ty T X) (erase t) (erasure ignores type substitution!) — under some atom valuation. Then we need to connect lr_{atom X ↦ C} (open_ty T X) (erase t) with lr_{0↦C} T (erase t) — a type-level de Bruijn/named correspondence, but now the *terms are identical* (erase t)! That's much better: we need a lemma about the relation on erased terms:

**Lemma (open_ty correspondence)**: For all types T (with bound vars), atom X, type U, valuations, lr_{ρ_atom, X↦C, ρ_bvar} (open_ty T U??) ... 

Hold on, we need lr_{atom X ↦ C} (open_ty T X) (erase t) ↔ lr_{bvar 0 ↦ C} T (erase t). This is a type-level "opening" lemma for the relation, with the same term on both sides. Provable by induction on T? Let's see: T = Ty_BVar 0: open_ty T X = Ty_FVar X, lr = C on both sides ✓. T = Ty_BVar (S i): open_ty = Ty_BVar (S i)?? Hmm, careful: `open_ty_rec 0 X T` replaces Ty_BVar 0, but the de Bruijn indices in T: under binders they shift. Let's just carefully define things. Since `open_ty_rec k U T` replaces index k, and under binders k increases. For T = Ty_BVar i at top level: open_ty_rec 0 X (Ty_BVar i) = if i =? 0 then X else Ty_BVar i. So for i=0: → Ty_FVar X; then lr_{atom} = ρ_atom X = C if valuation maps X to C ✓ matches lr_{bvar} = ρ_bvar 0 = C ✓. For i = S j: both sides Ty_BVar (S j) with ρ_bvar (S j) = (shift ρ_bvar) j... but wait, at the top level (no binder), the open_ty T X type has bvar indices unchanged for i≠0; the valuation for it should be ρ_bvar itself (interpreting the remaining free bvars), while T's valuation is 0↦C cons ρ_bvar. So for i = S j: lr_{0↦C;ρ_bvar} (Ty_BVar (S j)) = ρ_bvar j ✓ = lr_{ρ_bvar} (Ty_BVar (S j)) ✓. 

- T = Ty_FVar Y: unaffected by open_ty; both sides use ρ_atom Y — but the RHS valuation is 0↦C cons ρ_bvar plus ρ_atom, so it's ρ_atom Y ✓.
- T = Ty_Arrow A B: recursive ✓.
- T = Ty_All A: open_ty (Ty_All A) X = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 X A)?? Wait: open_ty_rec 0 X (Ty_All A) = Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S 0) X A) = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 X A). Hmm, that's not substituting the *bound* variable of this Ty_All (the bound variable inside A is index 0 relative to A, which is index... hmm). Let me recheck the definition:

Fixpoint open_ty_rec (k : nat) (U T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => if Nat.eqb k i then U else Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => Ty_FVar X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (open_ty_rec k U T1) (open_ty_rec k U T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S k) U T1)
  ...

So open_ty_rec k U T substitutes U for the *free* variable with de Bruijn index k, where "free index k at the top level" corresponds to index k counting binders crossed. So `open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T` substitutes for the outermost free variable (index 0 at top level). For T = Ty_All A: open_ty (Ty_All A) U = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U A). Inside A, the indices: bound by the Ty_All is index 0 (in A's local numbering), and the free variables of T correspond to indices ≥ 1 in A. So open_ty_rec 1 U A substitutes for the free variable that was index 0 at the outer level ✓ correct.

So the correspondence lemma: lr_{atom X↦C, bvar ρ} (open_ty_rec 0 (Ty_FVar X)?? ...). Hmm, we want: lr with the type T interpreted with bvar 0 ↦ C, versus the type open_ty T U interpreted with atom... The All case of the correspondence: T = Ty_All A, open_ty T X = Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 X A). The relation at Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 X A) under valuation ρ_atom(X↦C), ρ_bvar = ρ: ∀ C' candidate, lr_{bvar 0↦C', ρ_atom, ρ_bvar} (open_ty_rec 1 X A) (etapp u). And the RHS relation at Ty_All A under ρ_atom, bvar 0↦C: ∀ C', lr_{bvar 0↦C', bvar (i+1)↦...} A (etapp u), where the valuation for A is: 0 ↦ C', and shift of (0↦C, ρ). So RHS valuation inside: bvar 0 ↦ C', bvar 1 ↦ C, bvar (S (S i)) ↦ ρ i. LHS: under open_ty_rec 1 X A, the valuation (0↦C'; ρ_atom with X↦C; ρ_bvar). We need: interpreting `open_ty_rec 1 X A` where index 1 is replaced by X (which is interpreted as C) under the valuation [0↦C', ρ_atom, ρ_bvar] equals interpreting A under [0↦C', 1↦C, shifted ρ_bvar]. Hmm: in A, index 1 (free at the top level of A? no—) wait. Let's recompute: A has free indices relative to itself. In `open_ty_rec 1 X A`, index 1 of A is replaced by X. Hmm, the valuation for bvars when interpreting open_ty_rec 1 X A under [0↦C'] cons ρ_bvar: bvar (S j) ↦ ρ_bvar j. In A under [0↦C', 1↦C, shift ρ_bvar]: bvar (S (S i)) ↦ ρ_bvar i ✓ matches A's index S (S i) ↔ open_ty_rec's index... In open_ty_rec 1 X A, an occurrence of Ty_BVar (S (S i)) (i.e., ≥ 2) stays, and is interpreted as (cons C' ρ_bvar) (S i) = ρ_bvar i ✓. And A's Ty_BVar 1 ↦ C ✓ corresponds to X in the opened type ↦ ρ_atom X = C ✓. 

So the correspondence is a straightforward induction on the type, with the valuation shifting. This seems very manageable! And the terms are identical (erased).

But hold on: the erased-term approach requires the erased step relation and showing SN transfer. Let's think that through:

Define `etm` (erased terms): etm_bvar, etm_fvar, etm_abs etm, etm_app, etm_tabs etm, etm_tapp etm, etm_zero, etm_succ, etm_natrec. Erasure `erase : tm → etm`.

Define `estep : etm → etm → Prop` with the erasure of the step rules (dropping type arguments and lc conditions):
- app (abs b) v --> open_e b v when evalue v (or maybe no value condition?)
- app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2
- app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2'
- etapp (etabs b) --> b
- etapp t --> etapp t' when t --> t'
- succ, natrec rules.

Then prove: step t t' → estep (erase t) (erase t') — straightforward except the ST_* rules with lc hypotheses (which only make stepping harder in the annotated system, so erasure still steps ✓). Actually the erasure of tm_abs T t is etm_abs (erase t), so app (etm_abs (erase t)) (erase v) --> open_e (erase t) (erase v) = erase (open_tm t v)? Need open/erase commutation: erase (open_tm t v) = open_e (erase t) (erase v) ✓ (open_tm doesn't touch annotations; erase drops them). Similarly for tapp: erase (open_tm_ty t U) = erase t (since erasure drops annotations and open_tm_ty only changes annotations? Let's check open_tm_ty_rec: on tm_bvar/fvar unchanged; on abs: tm_abs (open_ty_rec...) (recursive); on tapp: tm_tapp (rec t1) (open_ty_rec ... T); so open_tm_ty only modifies annotations, not the term structure ✓. So erase (open_tm_ty t U) = erase t. And the erased step rule for etapp (etabs b) --> b matches ✓.

Then: SN_e (erase t) → SN (t)? We need: if the erased term is SN under estep, then the annotated term is SN under step. Proof: assume an infinite annotated reduction t = t0 --> t1 --> ...; erasing gives estep (erase ti) (erase t_{i+1}), an infinite erased reduction, contradiction ✓. So SN_e(erase t) → SN(t). And we need the fundamental theorem to give lr T (erase t) → SN_e (erase t) (the relation on erased terms implies erased SN). Then done. 

This avoids: annotation substitution issues, value/lc issues in the relation (they're in the annotated system, and the erased relation is coarser), and type substitution issues. But we need the erased relation to be sound: lr T (erase t) for well-typed t, with the fundamental theorem. The typing rules use opening with fresh type variables and term variables; the erasure of `open_tm t (tm_fvar x)` is `open_e (erase t) (efvar x)` ✓ (open_tm only affects term bvars; erase commutes). The erasure of `open_tm_ty t X` is erase t ✓.

The fundamental theorem on erased terms with a valuation of type variables (both bvar and fvar) by candidates:
- T_Var: t = fvar x, type T with lookup x Γ = Some T. Erased: efvar x. Need lr T (efvar x) under an environment interpretation. For the term-variable context interpretation, we could either use a substitution environment or interpret variables directly. Since erased variables: `efvar x` is neutral/stuck; lr T (efvar x) should hold for any T. If we prove "all stuck terms are related at all types" (neutral closure), then T_Var is easy without environments! But the typing rule T_Var requires lookup x Γ = Some T; the term is just a variable, which is stuck. So we don't even need the context interpretation for T_Var! Nice. But wait, the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case: body opened with a fresh term variable x; the hypothesis context is extended with x : T1. In the conclusion, we need lr (T1→T2) (eabs (erase body)). The definition: ∀ v ∈ lr T1, lr T2 (app (eabs (erase body)) v). The term app (eabs body) v steps to open_e body v when v is an *evalue*. If v is not an evalue, the app is... hmm, in the erased system we need to define when the beta step applies (with a value condition to mirror CBV). If v is not an evalue, the app only steps if the function steps (it's an eabs, a value, so no) — so it's stuck. Then lr T2 (app (eabs body) v) must hold for stuck terms. So we need the "stuck terms are related at all types" lemma, which requires that all lr relations contain stuck terms. Hmm, but "stuck" isn't a simple predicate: app (eabs b) v with v not an evalue is stuck; but is it related at all types? For T2 = arrow, its applications are stuck too... recursively. So we need a general lemma: terms that are SN, not evalues, and whose every one-step reduct is related at T, are related at T. This is the "E(T)" property discussed earlier, which needs induction on the type... and the All case needs lr at A[U]... 

Hmm, but with the erased/valuation approach, the All case is lr_{0↦C} A (etapp u) where A is a subterm — so structural induction on types works! Let's verify: E(T): sn t ∧ (∀t', estep t t' → lr T t') ∧ t not evalue → lr T t. Induction on T:
- Nat: sn ✓.
- Arrow A→B: need ∀v ∈ lr A, lr B (app t v). t not evalue → app t v not evalue (app is never an evalue in the erased system — evalues: eabs, etabs, numerals). Reducts of app t v: (a) app t' v for t --> t' — lr B since t' ∈ lr (A→B) ✓; (b) beta: only if t = eabs; but t is not an evalue and eabs is an evalue... in the erased system, is eabs always an evalue or only if lc? Let's define evalue (erased) as: eabs b is an evalue always, etabs b always, numerals always. Hmm, but then a non-lc annotated abs maps to an evalue eabs, and the annotated abs might not be a value. The erased step relation would let app (eabs b) v step, but the annotated term might not step. That's fine for soundness (erased steps are a superset of erased annotated steps; SN_e implies SN). But the relation lr on erased terms must be sound w.r.t. the erased step relation (which is coarser/denser). Since we only need SN_e → SN, a denser erased step relation is fine as long as lr T t → SN_e t and the fundamental theorem proves lr. So define erased evalues as: eabs, etabs, numerals — always. Then in case (b), if t = eabs b, then t IS an evalue, contradicting "t not evalue". So no beta case ✓. Similarly (c) app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' with v1 evalue: t is v1? t is the function of app t v, not the argument. The reducts of `app t v` where t is not an evalue: rules are app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 (t1 steps) — needs t step; and app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' (v1 evalue and t2 steps) — needs t evalue, no; and beta (eabs) — no. So reducts are exactly app t' v ✓. Then by IH E(B) applied to app t v: need sn (app t v) and all its reducts in lr B ✓. SN: t SN (given), v ∈ lr A → SN_e v; app t v SN? Its reducts are app t' v; need each SN: by "SN of application given SN of function and argument"? Hmm, we have t' ∈ lr (A→B) which gives t' v ∈ lr B → SN ✓ for each reduct t' of t. And also, the reducts are finitely... For SN of a term, we need all reducts SN. The reducts app t' v are SN because t' ∈ lr (A→B) → t' v ∈ lr B → SN_e ✓. So sn (app t v) ✓. Then apply E(B) with the reducts in lr B ✓. Great.
- All A: need ∀C candidate, lr_{0↦C} A (etapp t). t not evalue → etapp t not evalue (etapp is never an evalue). Reducts of etapp t: etapp t' for t --> t' (since t is not etabs; if t were etabs it'd be an evalue). Each lr_{0↦C} A (etapp t') ✓ since t' ∈ lr (All A) gives it. sn (etapp t) ✓ from those. Then E(A) with valuation 0↦C ✓ — A is a subterm ✓.

So E(T) is provable by structural induction on T with the erased/valuation design. 

Now, do we also need "neutral closure" for variables like efvar x (T_Var)? efvar x is not an evalue and has no reducts; E(T) applies with the vacuous reduct condition and sn ✓. So lr T (efvar x) for all T ✓. 

Now the term-variable environment: The fundamental theorem could be stated for *closed* contexts? No: T_Abs introduces a context with variables. Let's think about how to handle T_Abs with erasure:

Goal: has_type Δ Γ (tm_abs T1 body) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) → lr (T1→T2) (eabs (erase body)).
Definition: ∀ v, lr T1 v → lr T2 (eapp (eabs (erase body)) v).
Given v ∈ lr T1. Cases:
- If v is an evalue: eapp (eabs (erase body)) v --> open_e (erase body) v = erase (open_tm body v)... but here v is an *erased term*, not an annotated term! Oops. The relation is on erased terms, but the typing is about annotated terms. Hmm. The IH of the fundamental theorem for the body requires an annotated term substitution. We need to relate `open_e (erase body) v` (an erased term) to the erasure of some annotated term. If v = erase v0 for some annotated v0 with lr T1 (erase v0)... Hmm, the relation lr T1 is on erased terms, and v is an erased term that may not be the erasure of any well-typed annotated term. But the fundamental theorem's T_Abs needs: for ALL erased v ∈ lr T1, lr T2 (eapp (eabs (erase body)) v). So we need to prove lr T2 of `eapp (eabs b) v` for arbitrary v ∈ lr T1 where b = erase body. The reduct is `open_e b v`. Since the IH gives (for fresh x) lr T2 (open_e (erase body) (efvar x)) — using the typing premise at a fresh variable x (the term-variable context x : T1 is handled by... hmm, we need the fundamental theorem at the extended context). 

So we do need a context interpretation. The typing premise: ∀x ∉ L, has_type Δ (x|->T1; Γ) (open_tm body (fvar x)) T2. By IH: lr T2 (erase (open_tm body (fvar x))) = lr T2 (open_e (erase body) (efvar x)) given that the environment interprets x as related (efvar x ∈ lr T1 ✓ by E). Then we need to go from "∀ fresh x, lr T2 (open_e b (efvar x))" to "∀ v ∈ lr T1, lr T2 (open_e b v)". This requires a *term substitution lemma* for the erased relation: replacing a free variable by a related term.

Standard lemma: if for all fresh x, lr T2 (open_e b (efvar x)), then for all v ∈ lr T1, lr T2 (open_e b v) — because open_e b v = subst_e x v (open_e b (efvar x)) for fresh x, and lr T2 is closed under substitution of related terms for free variables. So we need:

**Lemma (semantic term substitution)**: if lr T u and [u has free var x?]... Actually the standard form: if lr T t and (for the free variable x, lr A (efvar x))... Hmm, the cleanest is the environment-based fundamental theorem:

**Fundamental theorem**: for all Δ Γ t T, has_type Δ Γ t T → for all σ : atom → etm, (∀ x A, lookup_context x Γ = Some A → lr A (σ x)) → lr T (subst_e σ (erase t))?

But then the substitution σ must commute with opening: subst_e σ (open_e b (efvar x)) vs open_e (subst_e (σ extended) b) ... Standard LN lemmas. Hmm.

Alternatively, prove a direct "substitution lemma" for lr:
**Lemma lr_subst**: For all T, t (erased), x, u, if (lr A (efvar x) for the type A? no...). The issue: t may have multiple free variables of different types; substituting u for one of them needs to know u's relation at the right type. The standard approach: prove the fundamental theorem with an environment that's a *term substitution* σ, and where the conclusion is lr T (σ t). Then:
- T_Var: t = fvar x, σ t = σ x, and the environment hypothesis gives lr T (σ x) ✓.
- T_Abs: t = abs T1 body; σ t = eabs (σ body) (substitution commutes with abs). Need ∀ v ∈ lr T1, lr T2 (eapp (eabs (σ body)) v). The IH gives, for the extended environment σ' = [x ↦ efvar x? or x ↦ v?]... Standard approach: prove the IH at the environment σ extended by x ↦ v for *each* v ∈ lr T1: the premise ∀x∉L, has_type ... (open_tm body (fvar x)) T2, and by IH with σ' = x ↦ v, other vars σ: lr T2 (σ' (erase (open_tm body (fvar x)))) = lr T2 (open_e (σ body) v) (using the standard lemma that substitution commutes with opening: σ'(open body x) = open (σ body) v when σ' extends σ with x ↦ v). ✓ Then the conclusion lr T2 (eapp (eabs (σ body)) v) follows from the "expansion" property: the app steps to the reduct (if v evalue) or is stuck (if v not evalue). Wait, if v is not an evalue, then the app is stuck (since eabs is an evalue, the only rule would be app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' requiring v (=t2) to step; hmm! if v is not an evalue but v steps, then app (eabs (σ body)) v --> app (eabs (σ body)) v' — so it does step, via the argument rule. So the reducts are: open_e (σ body) v (if v evalue) or app (eabs ...) v' for v --> v'. Both need to be lr T2. The first from the IH-with-substitution ✓; the second by induction on v's SN (v ∈ lr T1 → SN v) and the IH at v' (v' ∈ lr T1 by closure under step) — this is the "expansion"/E-type argument but for the specific term eapp (eabs b) v. We can handle it by induction on sn v: show lr T2 (eapp (eabs b) v) for all v ∈ lr T1, using E(T2)? But eapp (eabs b) v might be stuck or step; E(T2) requires "all reducts in lr T2 and not an evalue" — app is never an evalue ✓. So it suffices to show all reducts of eapp (eabs b) v are in lr T2 and it's SN. The reducts: open_e b v (if v evalue) ✓ from the substitution IH; app (eabs b) v' (if v --> v') — need lr T2 (app (eabs b) v'), which by induction on sn v (since v' ∈ lr T1 is SN with smaller derivation) ✓. And SN of the app: its reducts are SN (the first is lr T2 → SN; the others by IH → lr T2 → SN), and... to prove SN of a term it suffices that all one-step reducts are SN ✓ (that's the SN_intro constructor). So we can prove SN (eapp (eabs b) v) by showing all reducts SN. Then apply E(T2) ✓.

So the fundamental theorem with an environment σ, plus E(T), plus closure of lr under step, should work. And crucially, with erased terms, the type-substitution issue disappears because erasure makes type annotations irrelevant, and the All case is handled by a valuation of de Bruijn type variables.

   subst_e σ' (open_e b (efvar x)) = open_e (subst_e σ b) v.
This is the standard "substitution-open commutation": substituting a free variable x with efvar x then substituting σ' = σ[x↦v] equals substituting σ into b then opening with v. It holds because open_e b (efvar x) replaces the bound variable 0 with the free variable x, and then σ' substitutes x ↦ v; meanwhile subst_e σ b substitutes other free vars, then open with v replaces the bound var with v. They agree if σ' = σ ∪ {x ↦ v} and the bound variable doesn't clash. Standard lemma, needs care with the de Bruijn indices and the fact that v may contain free variables (σ only affects free vars; opening is fine). I'll prove it.

So the All case using the body + valuation is the key simplification: all type-level inductions are structural ✓.

Now, what exactly are the candidate conditions we need? Let's define:

Definition cand (P : etm → Prop) : Prop :=
  (∀ u, P u → SN_e u)                                           (* CR1 *)
  ∧ (∀ u u', P u → estep u u' → P u')                          (* CR2 *)
  ∧ (∀ u, (∀ u', estep u u' → P u') → P u)                     (* CR3: all reducts related → P — "expansion" *)
  
Hmm, should CR3 be unrestricted (no neutrality)? If P is closed under "all reducts related", then for a term that's an evalue with no reducts, we'd get P automatically — that's dangerous for arrow types (e.g., eabs with a bad body). So CR3 must be restricted to non-evalues (or neutral terms). Let's define:

  ∧ (∀ u, ¬ evalue u → (∀ u', estep u u' → P u') → P u)        (* CR3' *)

Is that enough? For lr of arrow types, CR3' requires: if u is not an evalue and all reducts are lr (A→B), then lr (A→B) u. Proof: need ∀v ∈ lr A, lr B (eapp u v). Since u is not an evalue, eapp u v is not an evalue; its reducts are eapp u' v (u steps) — lr B ✓ — and if v is an evalue... would eapp u v beta-step? Only if u = eabs, but u is not an evalue; in our erased system, is eabs the only evalue that can be a function position? evalues: eabs, etabs, numerals. An app of etabs/numeral is stuck; eabs → beta. So if u is not an evalue, no beta. So reducts are exactly eapp u' v ✓. And also, could eapp u v step by the argument rule (v steps)? That requires u to be an evalue — no. So reducts are eapp u' v ✓. Then to apply CR3' at B to eapp u v, we need all reducts in lr B ✓ (shown) and eapp u v not an evalue ✓ → lr B ✓. But CR3' at B is a property of lr B (the candidate), which we're trying to prove by induction on the type; so CR3' for arrow types is proven using CR3' for B ✓ (subterm). Good.

Also SN: lr (A→B) u includes sn u as a conjunct; the definition has sn u ∧ ∀v... ✓.

So the candidate properties are defined as a conjunction and we prove:
- cand_nat: cand (sn_e) — SN as a candidate? CR1 ✓; CR2: SN closed under step ✓; CR3': if u not evalue and all reducts SN then SN u ✓ (SN_intro).
- For arrow/All/Nat/var: proven by induction on the type, using properties of the component candidates.
- For Ty_BVar i and Ty_FVar X: ρB i and ρA X must be candidates — so the valuation must map to candidates. Thus the fundamental theorem's statement must quantify over candidate-valued valuations. Fine: `cand (ρA X)` and `cand (ρB i)` for all X, i.

Then `cand (lr ρ T)` is proven by induction on T, requiring cand of ρ at variables ✓.

Now, do we need `cand` for all the properties or can we derive them per-use? Having cand (lr ρ T) for all T (with candidate valuation) gives us the three properties uniformly.

Now let's nail the SN_e of application etc. Also, do we need "SN of evalue" etc.

### Erased term syntax

Actually, do we even need a separate erased syntax? Alternative: define the relation directly on annotated terms but ignoring annotations... The erasure approach requires defining a new syntax, a new step relation, and a whole parallel development (E(T), candidate properties, substitution lemmas, opening lemmas for erased terms). The annotated approach with a type valuation would need annotation-invariance lemmas instead. Which is less work?

With erasure, we need:
- erase function and its properties (commutation with open, subst).
- estep relation (9 rules) and evalue.
- SN_e with its lemmas (SN of app, etc.).
- The relation on etm and all the candidate lemmas (no lc issues!).
- Fundamental theorem.
- Transfer: SN_e (erase t) → SN t.

With the annotated approach with valuations, we'd need everything plus annotation invariance and lc lemmas. I think erasure is cleaner. The erased language is smaller (no lc conditions in steps). The main cost: duplication of term syntax and step rules, plus the erasure lemmas.

Actually, here's an even simpler idea: **work with an untyped/erased language where type annotations are dropped, but avoid defining a new inductive by... ** no, we need it.

Hmm, alternatively: define the relation on annotated terms but require the terms to be "erased-like"? No, let's do the erased syntax. Actually wait — maybe we can simplify further: define the erased terms as a *subset* of tm? No, tm_abs carries a type. We need a separate inductive.

Let me define:

Inductive etm : Type :=
  | etm_bvar : nat -> etm
  | etm_fvar : atom -> etm
  | etm_abs : etm -> etm
  | etm_app : etm -> etm
  | etm_tabs : etm -> etm
  | etm_tapp : etm -> etm
  | etm_zero : etm
  | etm_succ : etm -> etm
  | etm_natrec : etm -> etm -> etm -> etm.

Fixpoint erase (t : tm) : etm := ...

Inductive evalue : etm -> Prop :=
  | ev_abs : ∀b, evalue (etm_abs b)
  | ev_tabs : ∀b, evalue (etm_tabs b)
  | ev_zero : evalue etm_zero
  | ev_succ : ∀n, evalue n → evalue (etm_succ n).

Hmm, should etm_succ of a non-evalue be an evalue? In the annotated system, value (tm_succ n) is not a constructor — values are only abs, tabs, and numeric values. So `succ (app ...)` is not a value in the annotated system. In the erased system, mirroring: evalue (succ n) iff evalue n? Let's define evalue with succ only of evalues? Actually in the annotated language, value includes v_nat (numeric_value n), and numeric_value only includes zero and succ of numeric_value. So `succ t` is a value iff t is a numeric value; i.e., evalue (succ n) iff evalue n AND n is numeric. Simpler: define evalue := eabs | etabs | zero | succ evalue. Then evalue (succ (eabs b)) would be false ✓ matches (numeric_value can't contain abs). But `erase` of `succ (abs ...)` is `succ (eabs ...)`; in the annotated system, this is not a value; in the erased system, with evalue defined as succ of evalue, it's not an evalue ✓. Good.

Hmm, but the erased step rules: ST_Succ t t' → succ t --> succ t' — no value condition ✓. ST_RecZero requires value b, value s in the annotated system; in the erased system, should we require evalue b ∧ evalue s? If we include the value conditions in the erased step relation (mirroring), then erase of a step is an erased step ✓ (the annotated value conditions imply erased evalue conditions — need to prove: value v → evalue (erase v), and numeric_value n → evalue (erase n)). And SN_e (denser or sparser?) — if the erased step relation has *fewer* steps than the projection of annotated steps, then an infinite annotated reduction still projects to an infinite erased reduction (each annotated step projects to one erased step ✓ as long as the erased rule's conditions hold). So we should include whatever conditions make the projection work; including the value conditions is fine (they hold). But for the relation lr to be sound (lr → SN_e), the erased step relation's density matters: fewer erased steps makes SN_e easier, so it's fine. However, the candidate properties (CR2/CR3') and the E(T) arguments must be proven for the erased step relation as defined. If the erased steps have value conditions, then e.g. eapp (eabs b) v steps only when v is an evalue; if v is not an evalue, it's stuck ✓ (as discussed). And natrec: enatrec n b s steps when n is an evalue-zero or succ, b/s evalues, etc. This mirrors the annotated rules and is fine.

Hmm, but maybe simpler: define the erased step relation WITHOUT the value conditions (more steps, denser). Then SN_e is harder to prove, but the candidate/E(T) proofs may be simpler (fewer stuck cases). But the projection from annotated steps still works (each annotated step's erasure is an erased step if the erased relation is denser) ✓. Which is better? Let's think about the E(T) argument with the dense relation:
- eapp (eabs b) v always beta-steps (no evalue condition). Then eapp u v with u not an evalue but v a value... The reducts include the beta only if u = eabs; u not evalue → u ≠ eabs? If evalues include all eabs, yes ✓.
- The argument rule: eapp v1 t2 --> eapp v1 t2' when t2 steps — without the evalue condition, this would apply for any v1 (not just evalues). Then eapp u v with u not an evalue could step by the argument rule, giving eapp u v'. Hmm, in the E(T) proof at arrow type, we show ∀v ∈ lr A, lr B (eapp u v). The reducts of eapp u v (dense relation) include eapp u v' (v steps) — we'd need lr B (eapp u v'), which requires knowing u ∈ lr (A→B)... but that's what we're proving! Circular. With the value-conditioned argument rule (app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' only when v1 is an evalue), if u is not an evalue, no argument steps ✓. So the value-conditioned (CBV-style) erased relation is much easier for E(T). Let's mirror the annotated CBV rules.

So the erased step relation:
Inductive estep : etm -> etm -> Prop :=
  | EST_AppAbs : ∀b v, evalue v → estep (eapp (eabs b) v) (open_e b v)
  | EST_App1 : ∀u1 u1' u2, estep u1 u1' → estep (eapp u1 u2) (eapp u1' u2)
  | EST_App2 : ∀v1 u2 u2', evalue v1 → estep u2 u2' → estep (eapp v1 u2) (eapp v1 u2')
  | EST_TAppTabs : ∀b, estep (etapp (etabs b)) b
  | EST_TApp : ∀u u', estep u u' → estep (etapp u) (etapp u')
  | EST_Succ : ∀u u', estep u u' → estep (esucc u) (esucc u')
  | EST_RecArg : ∀n n' b s, estep n n' → estep (enatrec n b s) (enatrec n' b s)
  | EST_RecBase : ∀n b b' s, evalue n → evalue_special? ...

Hmm, the annotated rules:
  ST_RecBase : numeric_value n → b --> b' → lc s → natrec n b s --> natrec n b' s
  ST_RecStep : numeric_value n → value b → s --> s' → ...
  ST_RecZero : value b → value s → natrec zero b s --> b
  ST_RecSucc : numeric_value n → value b → value s → natrec (succ n) b s --> app (app s n) (natrec n b s)

For the erased versions, mirror:
  | EST_RecBase : ∀n b b' s, numeric_value_e n → estep b b' → estep (enatrec n b s) (enatrec n b' s)

  | EST_RecSucc : ∀n b s, numeric_value_e n → evalue b → evalue s → estep (enatrec (esucc n) b s) (eapp (eapp s n) (enatrec n b s))

where numeric_value_e mirrors numeric_value: nv_zero | nv_succ.

- ST_RecArg: n --> n' → estep (erase n) (erase n') → EST_RecArg ✓ (no extra conditions).
- ST_RecBase: numeric_value n → erase n is numeric_value_e ✓; b --> b' → EST_RecBase ✓.
- ST_RecStep: numeric_value_e (erase n) ✓; value b → evalue (erase b) ✓; EST_RecStep ✓.
- ST_RecZero: value b, value s → evalue ✓ → EST_RecZero ✓.
- ST_RecSucc: numeric_value n → numeric_value_e (erase n) ✓; evalue b, s ✓; the reduct erase (app (app s n) (natrec n b s)) = eapp (eapp (erase s) (erase n)) (enatrec (erase n) (erase b) (erase s)) ✓ matches.
- ST_AppAbs: lc condition irrelevant; value v → evalue (erase v) ✓; erase (open_tm t v) = open_e (erase t) (erase v) ✓.
- ST_App1: → EST_App1 ✓ (no lc needed).
- ST_App2: value v1 → evalue ✓.

- ST_TApp: ✓ no conditions.

Good. So erase_step: step t t' → estep (erase t) (erase t').

Then SN transfer: SN_e (erase t) → SN (t). Proven by: if t --> t' then estep (erase t) (erase t'), so an infinite step sequence maps to an infinite estep sequence; use the induction principle of strongly_normalizing: `strongly_normalizing t` iff all reducts SN. We can prove: SN_e u → ∀ t, erase t = u → SN t by induction on SN_e u; take t with erase t = u; for all t' with t --> t', we have estep (erase t) (erase t'), so SN_e (erase t') via the SN_e induction hypothesis, then apply IH... Let me define properly: 

Lemma sn_erase : ∀u, SN_e u → ∀t, erase t = u → strongly_normalizing t.
Proof: induction on SN_e u (i.e., SN_intro-style induction); intros t H; constructor; intros t' Hstep; apply IH with (u := erase t'); [apply H0; rewrite H; assumption | reflexivity].
Hmm, the SN_e induction gives: for all u' with estep u u', SN_e u' and (by the induction hypothesis as a function) ∀t, erase t = u' → SN t. So: SN t' follows from SN_e (erase t') and erase t' = erase t' ✓. But to get SN_e (erase t') we use the premise of SN_intro at u for the step estep u (erase t'), where u = erase t = erase t ✓ via H. 

Great, straightforward.

Then the final theorem: has_type [] empty t T → lr ρ0 T (erase t) → SN_e (erase t) → SN t ✓. Need a valuation ρ0 with candidates: use ρA := fun _ _ => True? Also the "empty" type context; the closed type T has no free type variables anyway, but the relation needs valuations. ✓.

T_Rec: t = tm_natrec n b s; T arbitrary. IH:
  IHn: lr Nat (erase n)
  IHb: lr T (erase b)
